## 5. The Budget Reframe — How to Change a Client's Mind About Investment

**Source:** Session 6, approx. 39:05–50:01 | Vimeo: 2026-02-18

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "They said, well, all we have is 100K. That's it. And I went and proposed something, and I said, by the way, I can help you with this, but this is what success looks like to me. I'm gonna exit by that time, but you're still gonna have those issues going da, da, da, possibly what's gonna happen is da, da, da, da. If you're okay with that, we can proceed. Said, okay, so what do you think we should be spending then? I said, for me, it's that much, but I would need to hire somebody who would be embedded in your teams that I will hire with you and train and be my shadow and hand over when I leave so you don't lose that. Well, that ended up being half a million."

**Pain point this addresses:**
Practice owners who accept the client's initial budget as a constraint — and as a result, either underdeliver (because the budget is not enough to do the work properly) or underprice themselves (because they adjust their scope to fit the budget rather than adjusting the budget to fit the scope).

**Value add:**
The budget reframe is the most commercially powerful technique in the GTL Programme for client engagement. It is not about negotiating — it is about helping the client understand the true cost of the problem they are trying to solve, and the true investment required to solve it properly. When done well, it is not a sales technique; it is a service.

**The framework — the budget reframe:**
1. **Accept the initial budget as a starting point**, not a constraint. "I can help you with this for 100K."
2. **Show what 100K gets them** — and what it does not get them. "By the time I exit, you will still have these issues."
3. **Ask the question** — "If you're okay with that, we can proceed. But what do you think we should be spending?"
4. **Propose the full solution** — with the rationale for the investment. "For me, it's X, but I would need to hire someone who would be embedded in your teams."
5. **Show the return** — "That ended up being half a million." The investment is justified by the outcome.

**Soundbite:**
> "They said 100K. I showed them what 100K gets. They said, what should we be spending? I said, half a million. They agreed."

**How-to / Practical application:**
1. When a client gives you a budget, accept it as a starting point. Do not reject it or immediately propose something bigger.
2. Show what the budget gets — and what it does not get. Be specific about the gaps.
3. Ask the question: "If you're okay with that, we can proceed. But what do you think we should be spending?"
4. Let the client answer. They will often surprise you.
5. Propose the full solution with the rationale. The investment is justified by the outcome.

**Tips, tricks, and nuances:**
- The budget reframe only works if you have done the scope-first work. Without scope clarity, you cannot show what the budget gets and what it does not get.
- The budget reframe is not about upselling — it is about alignment. The goal is to make sure that the client's investment matches the scope of the problem they are trying to solve.
- The budget reframe requires confidence. You need to be willing to say: "If you're okay with that outcome, we can proceed." Most practice owners are not willing to say this — and as a result, they accept budgets that set them up to fail.

**Domain:**
Practice — specifically, the sales effectiveness and offer architecture components of the practice-building roadmap. Also touches Potential (the budget reframe is what enables premium pricing).

**Connections to other IP:**
- Connects to the "Scope-First Principle" earlier in this session — the budget reframe is only possible after scope clarity.
- Connects to the "Bleeding Neck Problem" from Session 4 — the budget reframe works because the client can see the bleeding neck problem clearly.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "A client told me they had 100K. I walked out with half a million. Here's exactly how." Story: the budget reframe — how to help a client understand the true investment required to solve the problem they are trying to solve. CTA: "Are you accepting your clients' initial budgets as constraints? Or as starting points?"

# The GTL Roadmap Explained, The Safe Pair of Hands Ceiling, Presence Over Output, and the Skyscraper Syndrome — IP Extraction
**Source:** GTL Programme | Veronique | Session 7 | Date: TLI Onboarding Session (pre-Dec 2025, exact date not recorded in transcript)
**Dominant theme:** The TLI onboarding session — Jess walks Veronique through the GTL roadmap (the three pillars: Positioning, Practice, Potential), identifies her right next step (developing a compelling profile), and then has a breakthrough conversation about the "safe pair of hands" ceiling and the shift from output-driven work to presence-driven leadership.
**Secondary themes:** The skyscraper syndrome (one tall building surrounded by houses); the "problem solver" conditioning in corporate; the doctor-patient metaphor for presence vs. output; the dials metaphor for controlling your mode of engagement; the community hub as a digital learning journal.
**Sessions this theme also appears in:** Session 1 (the unspoken role — the first course Jess recommends in this session), Session 4 (the three lenses of positioning — the "you" lens is what the compelling profile work feeds into), Session 5 (the practice-building roadmap — this session is the first introduction to the three pillars).

## 1. The GTL Roadmap — The Three Pillars: Positioning, Practice, Potential

**Source:** Session 7, approx. 04:15–19:05 | Onboarding session

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "Positioning, practice and potential. Now, what is positioning? Positioning is how, for a very simple term, how people perceive you."
> "If I was to assign a weight to each one, they're all equal. However, the one with the lowest score is the one that's going to be dominant. It's the one that's going to put a ceiling on the other two."
> "A lot of programs and communities out there focus solely on practice. But that misses the point."

**Pain point this addresses:**
Leaders and practice owners who are investing heavily in one area (usually practice — skills, certifications, technical knowledge) while neglecting the other two (positioning and potential). The result is the skyscraper syndrome: one tall building surrounded by houses. The ceiling on growth is always the lowest-scoring pillar.

**Value add:**
The three-pillar GTL roadmap is the master framework for everything that happens in the programme. It is the map of the territory — and it is the first thing every new member needs to understand before they can make sense of the courses, the community, and the coaching. The key insight is the ceiling principle: you are only as strong as your weakest pillar.

**The framework — the three pillars:**

| Pillar | What it covers | The most common gap |
|---|---|---|
| Positioning | How people perceive you — on LinkedIn, in meetings, in the market. How you speak about yourself. | Most leaders have weak positioning: either a "menu" (everything they've ever done), or a positioning that hasn't shifted from corporate to practice. |
| Practice | The way you do work — mindset, influence, technical know-how, thought leadership. | Most leaders have strong technical practice but lack the influence and thought leadership components. |
| Potential | The intentionality and roadmap to grow — playing to strengths, building confidence, becoming a trusted advisor. | Most leaders are not investing in their potential deliberately. They are waiting for problems to activate them. |

**The ceiling principle:**
> "If you have an amazing practice and a great potential to grow, but your positioning — you're the world's best kept secret — then so what?"

The ceiling principle is the most important insight in the GTL roadmap. It explains why technically brilliant leaders get passed over, why experienced practitioners struggle to get clients, and why the most capable people in the room are often not the most influential. The ceiling is always the weakest pillar.

**The skyscraper syndrome:**
> "You've got a very tall, sophisticated building in a city. And then everything else is houses, houses, houses. Two-story, one-story houses. They have amazing practice, but then everything else is kind of there, not there. But for you to reach and break that ceiling, you need to have many tall buildings."

The skyscraper syndrome is the visual metaphor for the ceiling principle. It is the image of a person who has invested everything in one pillar — and as a result, has a very tall building (practice) surrounded by houses (positioning, potential). The goal of the GTL programme is to build all three pillars to equal height.

**Soundbite:**
> "The one with the lowest score is the one that's going to be dominant. It's the one that's going to put a ceiling on the other two. You can't break through a ceiling you can't see."

**How-to / Practical application:**
1. Map yourself against the three pillars. Where are you strongest? Where are you weakest?
2. Identify the ceiling: which pillar is putting a ceiling on the other two?
3. Focus the next 90 days on the weakest pillar — not the strongest. The strongest pillar does not need your attention right now.

**Tips, tricks, and nuances:**
- The three pillars are not independent — they are interdependent. Improving positioning will reveal gaps in practice. Improving potential will reveal gaps in positioning. They grow together.
- The most common mistake is investing in the strongest pillar (usually practice) because it feels good and produces visible results. The weakest pillar is where the leverage is.
- The ceiling principle applies at every stage of career and practice maturity. The same three pillars apply whether you are just starting or making $1 million a year — the depth and tactics change, but the pillars remain.

**Domain:**
This is the master framework for the entire GTL Programme. All other IP maps onto one or more of the three pillars.

**Connections to other IP:**
- Connects to the "Three Lenses of Positioning" from Session 4 — the positioning pillar made concrete.
- Connects to the "Practice-Building Roadmap" from Session 5 — the practice pillar made concrete.
- Connects to the "Why and Story" from Sessions 5 and 6 — the potential pillar made concrete.

**Diagram potential:**
Yes. A three-pillar visual: Positioning, Practice, Potential. Each pillar at equal height in the ideal state. A second visual showing the skyscraper syndrome: one tall pillar (Practice) and two short ones (Positioning, Potential). An arrow showing the ceiling that the short pillars impose on the tall one.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "Most leaders have one very tall building and two houses. Here's why that's the problem." Story: the three-pillar GTL roadmap — the ceiling principle and the skyscraper syndrome. CTA: "Which pillar is putting a ceiling on your growth right now?"

## 2. The Safe Pair of Hands Ceiling — Why Being Reliable Keeps You Stuck

**Source:** Session 7, approx. 20:01–22:16 | Onboarding session

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "I am very, very good at what I do. And the thing is, is that I can do a lot of things. And then so I'm a safe pair of hands. So people keep me there because I am a safe pair of hands. But then I cannot step up. And I know I can. But I cannot step up because people keep me in the box where I am making them safe."
> "When people say you're a safe pair of hands, I like it and I don't like it at the same time. It's obviously a praise, like it's a good thing to be a safe pair of hands. But it also dismisses your ability to lead."
> "It's like the difference between a nurse and a doctor. A nurse would be close to the work, the patient, and they would be a safe pair of hands, better be. But they're still the nurse. When something goes wrong, they don't turn to the nurse. They turn to the doctor."

**Pain point this addresses:**
Leaders who are highly competent, highly reliable, and highly valued — but who are stuck. They are the person everyone calls when something needs to be done well. They are trusted, respected, and appreciated. And they cannot move forward because their positioning has locked them into the "safe pair of hands" role.

**Value add:**
The "safe pair of hands" ceiling is one of the most powerful and resonant concepts in the GTL Programme. It names a phenomenon that many experienced leaders feel but cannot articulate. The insight is that being a safe pair of hands is not just a positioning problem — it is a self-reinforcing trap. The more reliably you deliver, the more people depend on you for delivery. The more people depend on you for delivery, the harder it is to move into the leadership and advisory roles.

**The nurse-doctor metaphor:**
The nurse-doctor metaphor is the most memorable illustration of the safe pair of hands ceiling. The nurse is close to the work, highly competent, and a safe pair of hands. But when something goes wrong, people turn to the doctor — not because the nurse is less capable, but because the doctor has a different positioning. The doctor is the decision-maker, the authority, the person who takes responsibility for the outcome.

The positioning shift from nurse to doctor is not about capability — it is about how you show up, how you speak about yourself, and what role you claim in the room.

**Jess's diagnosis:**
> "I love that you've mentioned that because everything you just said is all positioning. It's all positioning."

The safe pair of hands ceiling is a positioning problem, not a capability problem. Veronique is not stuck because she is not capable enough. She is stuck because her positioning — how she shows up, how she speaks about herself, what role she claims — has locked her into the delivery role.

**Soundbite:**
> "A safe pair of hands is a praise. But it also dismisses your ability to lead. When something goes wrong, they don't turn to the nurse. They turn to the doctor."

**How-to / Practical application:**
1. Identify the "safe pair of hands" signals in your current positioning: are you the person people call when something needs to be done? Or the person people call when they need to decide what to do?
2. The shift from "safe pair of hands" to "trusted advisor" is a positioning shift, not a capability shift. You do not need to become more capable — you need to show up differently.
3. The first step is to stop volunteering for the delivery role. When someone brings you a problem, resist the impulse to solve it immediately. Ask questions first.

**Domain:**
Positioning — specifically, the shift from delivery positioning to leadership/advisory positioning. Also touches Potential (the safe pair of hands ceiling is the most common ceiling in the GTL Programme).

**Connections to other IP:**
- Connects to the "Three Pillars" earlier in this session — the safe pair of hands ceiling is the positioning pillar at its lowest.
- Connects to the "Problem Solver Conditioning" in the next section — the safe pair of hands ceiling is caused by the problem solver conditioning.
- Connects to the "Five Positioning Roles" from Session 6 — the safe pair of hands is the "doing" role; the goal is to move to "advising" or "leading."

**Diagram potential:**
Yes. A visual showing the nurse-doctor spectrum: on the left, "safe pair of hands" (close to the work, delivery-focused, reactive); on the right, "trusted advisor" (strategic, decision-making, proactive). An arrow showing the positioning shift from left to right.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "Someone called me a 'safe pair of hands' last week. Here's why I didn't take it as a compliment." Story: the safe pair of hands ceiling — why being reliable keeps you stuck, and what it takes to make the shift. CTA: "Are you the nurse or the doctor in your organisation? And is that a choice you made — or a role you fell into?"

## 3. The Problem Solver Conditioning — Why Corporate Trains You to Stay Small

**Source:** Session 7, approx. 30:03–36:21 | Onboarding session

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "The conditioning in corporate is your value is attached to what have you done. Show me what's having fun."
> "Because we don't know what are the other levels, we're stuck into that one level of the production of outputs because we are incentivized in corporate to do that."
> "Shift that to: I've just had a conversation that unlocked the mindset of somebody and made them feel good about what they do. And as soon as the brain — but the brain won't realize until you yourself become present to the moment."

**Pain point this addresses:**
Leaders who have been trained by corporate environments to measure their value by their outputs — the documents, the presentations, the deliverables, the plans. When they move into advisory or leadership roles (or start a practice), they continue to produce outputs as proof of value — and as a result, they stay in the delivery role.

**Value add:**
The problem solver conditioning is one of the most important concepts in the GTL Programme for leaders who are transitioning from corporate to practice. It names the invisible force that keeps them in the delivery role — not lack of capability, not lack of ambition, but a deep conditioning that equates value with visible output.

**The two modes:**
1. **Problem solver mode** — you see a problem, you solve it, you produce an output. The output is the proof of your value. This is the corporate default.
2. **Presence mode** — you see a problem, you ask questions, you help the person find their own answer. The conversation is the value. This is the leadership/advisory default.

The shift from problem solver mode to presence mode is not a skill change — it is a conditioning change. And conditioning changes are slow, uncomfortable, and require deliberate practice.

Veronique's breakthrough example:
> "There were two things. One is I realized when I was jumping into problem solving mode. And at the same time, I had one of the letters saying, oh my god, Veronique, that was so helpful, thank you so much. And I was like, I haven't produced anything. It was just a conversation."

This is the moment of insight: the conversation was more valuable than any output she could have produced. And the conditioning — "I haven't produced anything" — was the thing that almost prevented her from having the conversation.

**Soundbite:**
> "The conditioning in corporate is your value is attached to what have you done. Show me the output. But the most valuable thing you can do is have a conversation that unlocks someone's thinking. And there's no document for that."

**How-to / Practical application:**
1. Notice when you are in problem solver mode. The signal is: you are already thinking about the output before you have fully understood the problem.
2. Practice the pause. When someone brings you a problem, resist the impulse to solve it immediately. Ask one question first.
3. After every interaction, ask yourself: did I produce an output, or did I have a conversation? Which one was more valuable?

**Tips, tricks, and nuances:**
- The problem solver conditioning is not a flaw — it is a feature that served you well in corporate. The goal is not to eliminate it, but to have conscious control over when you use it.
- The shift from problem solver to presence is gradual. You will not make it in one session. But every time you notice the conditioning and choose differently, you are building the new pattern.
- The most powerful signal that you are in presence mode: the other person says "that was so helpful" — and you did not produce a single document.

**Domain:**
Potential — specifically, the mindset and conditioning component. Also touches Practice (the shift from output-driven to presence-driven is a practice shift) and Positioning (the problem solver conditioning is what keeps leaders in the "safe pair of hands" positioning).

**Connections to other IP:**
- Connects to the "Safe Pair of Hands Ceiling" earlier in this session — the problem solver conditioning is the root cause of the safe pair of hands ceiling.
- Connects to the "Presence Over Output" in the next section — the presence mode is the antidote to the problem solver conditioning.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "I spent 20 years being rewarded for producing outputs. Here's what that training cost me." Story: the problem solver conditioning — why corporate trains you to stay small, and what it takes to unlearn it. CTA: "When was the last time you had a conversation that was more valuable than any document you could have produced?"

---

## 4. Presence Over Output — The Dials Metaphor and the Doctor-Patient Analogy

**Source:** Session 7, approx. 36:21–43:01 | Onboarding session

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "That's with presence. Being present in the moment. And you are the one in control of your dials, not your conditioning and subconscious programming."
> "When do I dial up on problem solving? Because we're in crisis mode. And when do I dial down and dial up on having a feel good — it's not a fluffy conversation, but feel good as in being grounded. There is hope, there is clarity and a mindset shift."
> "It's like an anxious doctor sitting in front of a patient. He's like, I feel this and I feel this and I feel this. It's like, I know, I know I'll give you the antibiotic. But what if you just wait, wait on the antibiotic bit, wait, listen to them and then say, I think I'm going to ask you a few questions."

**Pain point this addresses:**
Leaders who know they need to shift from output-driven to presence-driven — but do not know how. The dials metaphor gives them a practical, embodied way to think about the shift. The doctor-patient analogy gives them a concrete example of what presence looks like in practice.

**Value add:**
The dials metaphor and the doctor-patient analogy are the most practical tools in Session 7 for making the shift from problem solver mode to presence mode. They are not frameworks or processes — they are images that help the leader feel the difference between the two modes, and give them a way to consciously choose which mode to be in.

**The dials metaphor:**
> "You are in control of your dials. When do I dial up on problem solving? Because we're in crisis mode. And when do I dial down and dial up on having a feel good — it's not a fluffy conversation, but feel good as in being grounded."

The dials metaphor reframes the shift from problem solver to presence as a choice — not a transformation. You are not becoming a different person. You are choosing which dial to turn up and which to turn down, based on what the situation actually needs.

The two dials:
- **Problem solving dial** — turn up in crisis mode, when there is a genuine need for immediate output.
- **Presence dial** — turn up when the person needs to be grounded, when they need clarity, when they need a mindset shift.

The key insight: most leaders have the problem solving dial permanently turned up. The goal is not to turn it off — it is to have conscious control over when you turn it up.

**The doctor-patient analogy:**
> "It's like an anxious doctor sitting in front of a patient. He's like, I feel this and I feel this and I feel this. It's like, I know, I know I'll give you the antibiotic. But what if you just wait, wait on the antibiotic bit, wait, listen to them and then say, I think I'm going to ask you a few questions."

The anxious doctor is the problem solver in action: they hear the symptoms, they immediately diagnose, they prescribe the antibiotic. The patient leaves with a prescription — but not with clarity, not with grounding, not with the sense that they have been truly heard.

The present doctor waits. They ask questions. They listen. They give the patient something to relieve the immediate pain — and then they ask them to come back. The patient leaves with a much better outcome.

The analogy is not about being slow or withholding help. It is about the quality of the help. The present doctor gives better help — not less help.

**Soundbite:**
> "You are in control of your dials. Not your conditioning. Not your subconscious programming. You. When do you dial up on problem solving? When do you dial up on presence? That's the work."

**How-to / Practical application:**
1. Before your next meeting or interaction, set an intention: which dial am I going to turn up today?
2. In the meeting, notice when the problem solving dial starts to turn up automatically. That is the conditioning. You do not have to follow it.
3. Practice the doctor-patient move: when someone brings you a problem, ask one question before you offer a solution. Just one. See what happens.

**Tips, tricks, and nuances:**
- The dials metaphor is most useful in high-stakes situations where the problem solving impulse is strongest. Those are exactly the situations where presence is most valuable.
- The doctor-patient analogy is most useful for change managers and consultants who are used to being the "expert with the answer." The shift to "expert with the question" is uncomfortable at first — but it is the shift that changes the positioning.
- The goal is not to eliminate the problem solving dial. It is to have conscious control over it. There are situations where immediate output is exactly what is needed. The skill is knowing the difference.

**Domain:**
Potential — specifically, the mindset and presence component. Also touches Practice (presence is a practice skill — it requires deliberate development) and Positioning (the shift from output-driven to presence-driven changes how people perceive you).

**Connections to other IP:**
- Connects to the "Problem Solver Conditioning" earlier in this session — the dials metaphor is the practical tool for overcoming the conditioning.
- Connects to the "Safe Pair of Hands Ceiling" earlier in this session — presence is what breaks the ceiling.
- Connects to the "Shining Light" metaphor from Session 5 — presence is the inner state that enables the shining light.

**Diagram potential:**
Yes. A visual showing two dials: "Problem Solving" and "Presence." Both dials on a spectrum from 0 to 10. A second visual showing the default state (Problem Solving at 9, Presence at 2) vs. the goal state (both at 5, with conscious control over which to turn up based on context).

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "I used to walk into every meeting with the answer already prepared. Here's what I learned when I stopped." Story: the dials metaphor and the doctor-patient analogy — the shift from output-driven to presence-driven leadership. CTA: "Which dial do you have permanently turned up? And is that a choice — or a habit?"

**Reel / YouTube video idea:**
Opening line: "The anxious doctor gives you the antibiotic before you finish your sentence. The present doctor asks you one question — and changes everything." 60-second arc: the anxious doctor (problem solver mode) → the present doctor (presence mode) → the dials metaphor → close with "You are in control of your dials. Not your conditioning."

---

## 5. The Positioning Shift — From Corporate to Practice (The Menu Problem)

**Source:** Session 7, approx. 05:55–08:50 | Onboarding session

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "The biggest shift is when you shift from corporate to having your own solo consulting business. This is a different positioning. It builds on the positioning that you already have. But the way you speak about yourself changes."
> "They talk about themselves exactly the same as they would be doing in an interview. Then they say, why don't I end up getting the gig? Or why, after all this talk, they tell me, you know what, why don't you just come and work with us as a contractor?"
> "In some cases, the positioning is not good at all, in the sense that it's a menu. That's everything I have ever done for the past 20 years. Surely they can see the value. Why am I not getting the role?"

**Pain point this addresses:**
Leaders who are transitioning from corporate to consulting/practice and who are presenting themselves exactly as they would in a job interview — with a comprehensive CV, a long list of skills and experiences, and a "menu" of everything they can do. The result is that clients either do not understand what they do, or they offer them a contractor role instead of the consulting engagement they were pitching for.

**Value add:**
The "menu problem" is one of the most common and most costly positioning mistakes in the GTL Programme. It names the specific failure mode that prevents experienced leaders from landing consulting engagements — and it makes clear that the solution is not more experience or more credentials, but a fundamentally different way of speaking about yourself.

**The menu problem:**
A menu positioning is: "I have 20 years of experience in change management, project management, stakeholder engagement, organisational design, culture change, digital transformation, and leadership development." It is comprehensive. It is accurate. And it is completely ineffective as a consulting positioning.

The problem is not that the person has done all of those things. The problem is that a menu positioning does not tell the client what problem you solve, who you solve it for, or what makes you different from every other experienced leader with a similar menu.

**The corporate-to-practice shift:**
The positioning that works in corporate (comprehensive, broad, "I can do many things") is the positioning that fails in practice (specific, focused, "I solve this specific problem for this specific person"). The shift is not about hiding your experience — it is about curating it. You are not presenting everything you have done; you are presenting the thread that connects everything you have done to the specific problem you solve.

**Soundbite:**
> "The menu is everything you've ever done for 20 years. Surely they can see the value. But they don't. Because a menu is not a positioning. It's a list."

**How-to / Practical application:**
1. Read your current LinkedIn profile or bio. Is it a menu? Or is it a positioning?
2. The test: can someone read your profile and immediately understand (a) what problem you solve, (b) who you solve it for, and (c) what makes you different? If not, it is a menu.
3. The fix: identify the one thread that connects everything you have done. That thread is your positioning. Everything else is context.

**Domain:**
Positioning — specifically, the corporate-to-practice positioning shift. This is the most common positioning failure mode in the GTL Programme.

**Connections to other IP:**
- Connects to the "Three Lenses of Positioning" from Session 4 — the menu problem is the failure to apply the three lenses.
- Connects to the "Positioning Matrix" from Session 5 — the positioning matrix is the tool for moving from a menu to a positioning.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "I had 20 years of experience. My LinkedIn profile was a menu. Here's what I changed." Story: the menu problem — why a comprehensive list of everything you've done is the worst possible positioning for a consulting practice. CTA: "Is your profile a menu or a positioning? Read it again and tell me what problem you solve."

# The Wrong Tent Problem, The Double Contract, and the Faster and Better Positioning — IP Extraction
**Source:** Elevate Programme | Cheryl | Session 1 | Date: 16 February 2026 | Vimeo: TLI-[Gallery View] 1 on 1 with Cheryl 2026-02-16
**Dominant theme:** How an internal change and transformation practitioner navigates a structurally ambiguous role in a large organisation — specifically: how to position yourself when no one knows what your role is, how to contract with the right stakeholders, and how to avoid the trap of operating outside your tent of influence before you have credibility inside it.
**Secondary themes:** The arms-and-legs trap (being used as a delivery resource rather than a strategic partner); the insecure leader dynamic and how to manage upward without triggering threat responses; the "faster and better" value proposition as a positioning anchor; the use-case approach to building external credibility; the integration imperative (not operating at a level so far above the team that you inadvertently isolate yourself).
**Sessions this theme also appears in:** Session 2 (the co-funding model, the punch drunk organisation, the 90-day window — all build on the stakeholder navigation foundations laid here).

---

## Overview

Session 1 with Cheryl is the context-setting and game-plan session. Cheryl is eight weeks into a secondment role at Origin Energy's Generation and Energy Transition (GET) division — a 500–600-person division in the middle of a leadership transition, with an outgoing EGM, an incoming leader from the gas division, and a direct boss (Paul) who is insecure, non-confrontational, and at risk of being displaced. Cheryl's mandate is to embed business improvement practices into the division, but the role has no clear definition, no established credibility, and no external-facing engagement yet.

The coaching session moves through three phases: (1) context mapping — Jess listens and maps the landscape; (2) diagnosis — Jess identifies the key structural and relational risks; (3) game plan — Jess gives Cheryl a concrete set of moves for the next four to six weeks.

The IP in this session is dense. Jess articulates several frameworks in real time — some named, some unnamed but clearly distinct — that are immediately applicable to any practitioner navigating an internal consulting or transformation role in a large organisation.

---

## Framework 1: The Wrong Tent Problem

**What it is:** The Wrong Tent Problem describes the mistake of spending your influence capital outside your immediate team (the "tent") before you have established credibility and alignment inside it. Cheryl had been doing excellent work — designing workshops, creating a services menu, building a 12-month roadmap — but presenting it externally before her own team and direct leader had fully endorsed it. Jess identifies this as a structural risk: it creates the impression of going rogue, undermines the leader's authority, and makes the practitioner look like a solo operator rather than a team member.

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "I would caution you that in the next couple of months until they get that strategic planning cycle and stuff, is not to play fully solo, try to bring some of your team members, increase your communication with your direct boss just because although he might be at risk of losing the job unfortunately — or just playing a vanilla version. Like it's just playing vanilla at the moment."

**The distinction:** The Wrong Tent Problem is not about being too good or too fast. It is about sequencing. You must win the tent before you win the room. The tent is your boss, your peers, your immediate team. The room is the business unit leaders, the sponsors, the external stakeholders. If you go to the room before you have the tent, the tent will undermine you — not necessarily through active sabotage, but through the absence of endorsement.

**The fix:** Cheryl's move is to bring Paul (her direct boss) into the loop explicitly — not as a courtesy, but as a deliberate act of giving him back the authority she inadvertently took from him by presenting the services menu in a group setting without him. The framing: "I'm part of your team and the success of me and others is all gonna be compounded to your success. So I don't want to seem like I'm stepping on toes or doing things that is not aligned. So I'm genuinely looking for your feedback and if you're happy for me to go ahead with what I've proposed, then we go at it as a team."

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "Most internal consultants get this wrong. They go to the business before they've won their own team. Here's the sequencing that actually works."
- Workshop module: Stakeholder Sequencing — the tent before the room
- Book chapter: The Wrong Tent Problem as a chapter on internal influence

**Priority:** High

---

## Framework 2: The Double Contract

**What it is:** The Double Contract is the principle that any internal practitioner operating in a matrix or cross-functional environment has two contracts to manage simultaneously: the contract with the sponsor (the person who hired them or gave them the mandate) and the contract with the team (the people they actually need to work with to deliver value). Most practitioners manage only the first contract and wonder why the second one is always broken.

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "The key for the first maybe three [months] — you need to be very, very deliberate even to the point of rehearsing. What is the value that you bring? You're not there to help them deliver. You're there to help them. When this change happens again, you do it faster and better."

**The distinction:** The Double Contract is not just about stakeholder management. It is about the nature of the commitment. The sponsor contract is about mandate and resources. The team contract is about skin in the game — what the practitioner will give and what they need in return. Jess articulates this explicitly: "This is not me going off to do solo work for you. I do it with you, not for you or to you."

**The canvas reference:** Jess references a canvas she uses with clients that has a specific section for this contract — "this is what I'm gonna help you with, but this is what I need from you as well." This is a named tool within Jess's methodology.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "Most consultants think they have one contract. They actually have two. And the second one is the one that determines whether they succeed." (This verbatim hook was already identified in the previous extraction — it is confirmed here as the correct framing.)
- Workshop exercise: The Double Contract canvas — fill in both sides before you start any engagement
- Elevate programme module: Contracting for internal practitioners

**Priority:** High

---

## Framework 3: The Faster and Better Value Proposition

**What it is:** "Faster and Better" is the positioning anchor Jess gives Cheryl for every stakeholder conversation. It is a one-line value proposition that does three things simultaneously: (1) it positions Cheryl as a capability builder, not a delivery resource; (2) it implies that the team will be able to operate without her (which removes the threat of dependency); and (3) it anchors the value in a future state that every leader wants.

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "When you do this yourself next time, you're doing it faster and better. That's what I do. That's it. Like that is the line, your tagline when you — when you guys do this yourself next time you do it faster and better. So that positions you: I'm in, out, build capability, bring clarity, get momentum, get traction. And when I go, you guys already have that kind of capability so you could do it faster and better."

**The distinction:** "Faster and Better" is not about speed or quality in isolation. It is about the transfer of capability. The practitioner is not there to do the work for the team. They are there to do the work with the team in a way that leaves the team permanently more capable. This is the difference between a contractor and a consultant, and between a consultant and a coach.

**Why it works:** No leader will say no to "faster and better." It is non-threatening (you are not taking over), it is outcome-focused (it implies measurable improvement), and it is self-limiting (it implies you will leave when the job is done). It also positions the practitioner as a peer of the leader, not a resource of the team.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "Your value proposition as an internal consultant should be one sentence. Here's the one I give every client."
- Talk/keynote: The Capability Builder vs. the Delivery Person
- Elevate programme: Positioning for internal practitioners — the one-line value proposition

**Priority:** High

---

## Framework 4: The Arms and Legs Trap

**What it is:** The Arms and Legs Trap is the pattern where a practitioner with strategic capability gets used as a delivery resource — doing the work for the team rather than building the team's capability to do the work themselves. Cheryl's predecessor in the role had fallen into this trap: "the customer loved it" but the work was never strategic, never tracked, never visible at the leadership level. It was just "arms and legs."

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "Basically arms and legs, but it wasn't really a job like with a proper structure."

**The distinction:** The Arms and Legs Trap is not about the quality of the work. The predecessor was doing good work. The trap is about positioning. When you are positioned as arms and legs, you are positioned as a resource — and resources are managed, not respected. They are used when needed and cut when budgets tighten. The strategic practitioner needs to be positioned as a partner, not a resource.

**The fix:** Jess's prescription is the use-case approach — going into each business unit conversation with five specific use cases of where Cheryl can add value, framed not as "I can help you deliver" but as "here is what success looks like for you, and here is how I help you get there faster and better." This shifts the conversation from resource allocation to strategic partnership.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "The arms and legs trap. Why smart practitioners get stuck doing work that doesn't reflect their capability."
- Workshop module: Positioning for internal practitioners — moving from resource to partner
- Elevate programme: The use-case approach to stakeholder engagement

**Priority:** Medium

---

## Framework 5: The Insecure Leader Dynamic

**What it is:** The Insecure Leader Dynamic is the pattern where a direct boss who is insecure in their own role responds to a high-performing direct report not with support but with surveillance. Jess identifies this in Paul (Cheryl's direct boss) immediately: "I suspect there's two things happening for him. One insecure, two doesn't really know what he's doing. And that means two things when you put together the slide for the services, which is as you've mentioned, has been well received. It's where received. But it's also a threat, a direct threat to Paul."

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "He is gonna be a wink. He's gonna be, I want to be in every conversation. He is gonna be watching your every move, which puts a little bit of a — is that nice? Not nice feeling."

**The distinction:** The Insecure Leader Dynamic is not about the leader being bad. Paul is described as "lovely" and "giving." The dynamic is structural: when a direct report is visibly more capable than their boss, the boss's insecurity is triggered not by malice but by self-preservation. The practitioner's job is to manage this dynamic proactively — not by hiding their capability, but by consistently framing their work as a contribution to the boss's success.

**The fix:** Give the boss back what you inadvertently took from them. In Cheryl's case, this means a one-on-one with Paul before the next external presentation, framed as: "I'm part of your team and the success of me and others is all gonna be compounded to your success."

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "The insecure leader dynamic. What to do when your boss is threatened by your capability."
- Coaching framework: Managing upward — the three moves for navigating an insecure leader
- Elevate programme: Stakeholder navigation — the boss as a stakeholder

**Priority:** Medium

---

## Framework 6: The Integration Imperative

**What it is:** The Integration Imperative is the principle that a practitioner who operates at a level significantly above their team's capability will, by default, isolate themselves from the team — even if they are doing excellent work. Jess articulates this as a structural risk: "If you're operating at level five and they're at level one, you have by default taking yourself out of the team. You're not integrating with the team."

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "In doing that, you risk doing exactly the opposite of everything you've been talking about, which is integration. Because if you're operating at level five and they're at level one, you have by default taking yourself out of the team."

**The distinction:** The Integration Imperative is not about dumbing down. It is about sequencing and pacing. The practitioner needs to bring the team along — not by slowing down their own work, but by creating visible touchpoints where the team is included, credited, and developed. The goal is to be seen as a team player who happens to be excellent, not as an individual contributor who happens to be in a team.

**The fix:** Tag-team approach — Cheryl explicitly tells her team to grab her and go into meetings together. The goal is to form a cohesive unit that is built around Cheryl, supported by the business, but also visibly supported by the team.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "The integration paradox. Why being too good can isolate you from the team you're trying to help."
- Coaching framework: The integration imperative — how to bring your team along without slowing down
- Elevate programme: Team dynamics for internal practitioners

**Priority:** Medium

---

#### The 90-Day Window, The Punch Drunk Organisation, The Co-Funding Model, and Seed Planting — IP Extraction (Cheryl Session 2)

# The 90-Day Window, The Punch Drunk Organisation, The Co-Funding Model, and Seed Planting — IP Extraction
**Source:** Elevate Programme | Cheryl | Session 2 | Date: 6 March 2026 | Vimeo: TLI-[Gallery View] 1 on 1 with Cheryl 2026-03-06
**Dominant theme:** How a practitioner navigates an organisation in structural chaos — specifically: how to create strategic momentum when the leadership is distracted, how to plant seeds of influence that will pay off when the dust settles, and how to use the co-funding model to get resources and visibility without waiting for budget approval.
**Secondary themes:** The punch drunk organisation (a leadership team so overwhelmed by change that they cannot make decisions); the 90-day window (the critical period before a new leader consolidates their position); the capability builder vs. delivery person distinction (confirmed and deepened from Session 1); the cadence and commitment model for internal consulting engagements; the ambassador effect (direct reports as advocates to the sponsor).
**Sessions this theme also appears in:** Session 1 (the Wrong Tent Problem, the Double Contract, the Faster and Better positioning — all foundational to the strategies deployed in this session).

---

## Overview

Session 2 takes place approximately three weeks after Session 1. Cheryl has just returned from a week-long site visit to Origin Energy's Eraring plant in Newcastle — the company's largest coal-fired power station, scheduled to close in 2029. The visit was a deliberate move to build relationships with the operational leadership team (the direct reports of Anthony, the group manager who is the front-runner for the top divisional role). The session is a debrief of that visit and a forward-planning conversation for the next phase of Cheryl's influence strategy.

The coaching session is dense with strategic insight. Jess articulates several frameworks in real time — some named, some unnamed but clearly distinct — that are immediately applicable to any practitioner navigating an organisation in transition.

---

## Framework 1: The Punch Drunk Organisation

**What it is:** The Punch Drunk Organisation describes a leadership team that has been hit by so many changes in quick succession that they have lost the capacity to think strategically or make decisions. They are not incompetent — they are overwhelmed. The result is a pattern of deferred decisions, cancelled meetings, and a general inability to act on good work that has been presented to them.

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "They're just, they're a bit punch drunk at the moment. No one's thinking straight. So I'm just trying to look for smart ways for them to present themselves and their value that they are."

**The context:** Cheryl's leadership team had received her 12-month roadmap and engagement strategy but had not acted on them. They had promised to share the material with the broader team but had deferred. Jess's diagnosis: this is not a rejection of the work. It is a symptom of the Punch Drunk Organisation — a team so overwhelmed by leadership transitions (the EGM retiring, the incoming leader from the gas division, Paul's own uncertain future) that they cannot process new information, even when it is excellent.

**The distinction:** The Punch Drunk Organisation is not a permanent state. It is a transitional state — and it has a window. Once the new leader consolidates their position and the dust settles, the organisation will be ready to act. The practitioner's job during the punch drunk phase is not to push harder but to plant seeds — to create the conditions for action when the organisation is ready.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "The punch drunk organisation. What to do when the leadership team is too overwhelmed to act on good work."
- Workshop module: Navigating organisational chaos — the practitioner's playbook
- Elevate programme: Stakeholder navigation during leadership transitions

**Priority:** High

---

## Framework 2: The 90-Day Window

**What it is:** The 90-Day Window is the critical period between the announcement of a new leader and the consolidation of their position. During this window, the new leader is still forming their view of the organisation, still deciding who to trust, still looking for quick wins. This is the practitioner's best opportunity to get in front of the new leader with a clear value proposition and a demonstrated track record.

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "By the time all of this announcement happens and then, you know, let it settle, settle for a bit, then you will, you know, you would have had planted seeds everywhere."

**The context:** Anthony (the group manager) has not yet been formally announced as the new EGM, but is the front-runner. Cheryl has been building relationships with his direct reports — the operational leaders at the Eraring site. Jess's strategic insight: the work Cheryl is doing now with the direct reports is not just about building those relationships. It is about creating the conditions for Anthony to hear about Cheryl's work from his own team — which is a far more powerful endorsement than anything Cheryl could say directly.

**The distinction:** The 90-Day Window is not about timing a pitch. It is about positioning. The practitioner who has planted seeds with the new leader's team before the leader consolidates their position is already positioned as a trusted partner — not a vendor or a resource. The window closes when the new leader has formed their view. After that, it is much harder to shift the positioning.

**The sequencing:** Cheryl's approach — building relationships with the direct reports first, then letting those relationships speak to Anthony — is the correct sequencing. It is the opposite of going directly to the top, which would be premature and potentially threatening to Paul.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "The 90-day window. Why the most important thing you can do when a new leader arrives is not to pitch them."
- Workshop module: Navigating leadership transitions — the 90-day playbook
- Elevate programme: Strategic positioning during organisational change

**Priority:** High

---

## Framework 3: The Co-Funding Model

**What it is:** The Co-Funding Model is a resource acquisition strategy where the practitioner identifies a shared interest between two budget holders and proposes a joint investment that benefits both. Cheryl uses this in real time during Session 2: she negotiates with the technology team to co-fund a digital workshop experiment, framing it as a mutual benefit — the technology team gets to demonstrate that they are serving the business better, and Cheryl's team gets the resource to run the experiment.

**Verbatim quote (Cheryl):** "I said, would you co-fund an experiment like that here? I said, the wallets aren't so deep here for that type of exercise, but if we went joint, you could show that you are serving the business better. We get utility for you, and we sing each other's praises. Everybody wins."

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "Very good. Very good. And by the time all of this announcement happens and then, you know, let it settle, settle for a bit, then you will, you know, you would have had planted seeds everywhere."

**The distinction:** The Co-Funding Model is not just a budget hack. It is a relationship-building strategy. By bringing the technology team into the experiment, Cheryl creates an ally — someone who has a stake in her success and who will speak positively about her work in their own network. It also demonstrates strategic thinking: Cheryl is not just solving her own problem, she is creating value for the technology team at the same time.

**The principle:** Find the overlap between your need and someone else's mandate. Propose a joint investment that serves both. The result is not just a resource — it is an ally, a co-investor, and a future ambassador.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "The co-funding model. How to get resources when your budget is thin."
- Workshop module: Creative resource acquisition for internal practitioners
- Elevate programme: Building alliances — the co-funding approach

**Priority:** High

---

## Framework 4: Seed Planting as a Strategic Posture

**What it is:** Seed Planting is the deliberate act of creating conditions for future influence — not by pushing for immediate action, but by consistently demonstrating value, building relationships, and creating positive associations that will pay off when the organisation is ready to act. Jess names this explicitly in the session: "You would have had planted seeds everywhere."

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "So I just, I'm just trying to pick the conscience here and there where I can. So that's part of the engagement, trying to show them. Yeah. Yeah. So yeah, that, that a business person, business improvement person is, can be more strategic and thinking for them."

**The context:** Cheryl is operating in a punch drunk organisation during a 90-day window. The correct posture is not to push for decisions or commitments. It is to plant seeds — to create the conditions for action when the organisation is ready. Cheryl's week at Eraring is a seed-planting exercise: she is not there to close deals, she is there to build relationships, demonstrate her thinking, and create positive associations with her work.

**The distinction:** Seed Planting is not passive. It is a deliberate, strategic posture. The practitioner who plants seeds is not waiting — they are actively creating the conditions for future influence. The difference between seed planting and waiting is intentionality: every interaction is designed to create a positive association, build a relationship, or demonstrate a capability.

**The ambassador effect:** Jess identifies the mechanism by which seed planting pays off: the direct reports become ambassadors. "You will be presenting that to the team and to the stakeholder, to the sponsor, to showcase what you have delivered so far. And it's best obviously that they can speak themselves, the direct reports." When the sponsor (Anthony) hears about Cheryl's work from his own team, the endorsement is far more powerful than anything Cheryl could say directly.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "Seed planting as a strategic posture. Why the most powerful influence move is often the one that doesn't look like an influence move."
- Workshop module: The ambassador effect — how to turn direct reports into advocates
- Elevate programme: Influence strategy for internal practitioners

**Priority:** High

---

## Framework 5: The Cadence and Commitment Model

**What it is:** The Cadence and Commitment Model is the practitioner's tool for structuring an internal consulting engagement so that both parties know what they are committing to, what progress looks like, and how they will communicate. Jess articulates this in response to Cheryl's description of her upcoming one-on-one meetings with the operational leaders at Eraring.

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "It would be very important to create that cadence because first people forget, people will measure only on the end goal, which you may not have and certainly will not have a hundred percent control over. Sure. And third, you might be in a position of comparison with the lady, even though you're offering something different. So to remove all of that and de-risk it. You would need to define what, not just what good looks like, but what is progress and what's the cadence. When do we meet, how do we talk, how do we align all of that?"

**The three elements:**
1. **The commitment** — what the practitioner will give and what they need in return ("skin in the game")
2. **The progress definition** — not just the end goal, but what progress looks like at each stage
3. **The cadence** — how often they meet, how they communicate, how they measure alignment

**The canvas reference:** Jess references the canvas from Session 1 again — specifically the lower part of the canvas that covers cadence and commitment. This is a confirmed named tool in Jess's methodology.

**The de-risking principle:** The Cadence and Commitment Model de-risks the engagement in three ways: it prevents the "I forgot what we agreed" problem; it prevents the "I'm only measuring you on the end goal" problem; and it prevents the comparison problem (being compared to the previous practitioner who "just did things for people").

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "The cadence and commitment model. Why every internal consulting engagement needs a contract — and what that contract should include."
- Workshop exercise: The engagement canvas — fill in the commitment and cadence sections before you start
- Elevate programme: Structuring internal consulting engagements

**Priority:** Medium

---

## Framework 6: The Capability Builder vs. Delivery Person Distinction (Confirmed)

**What it is:** This framework was introduced in Session 1 as the "Faster and Better" value proposition. Session 2 deepens and confirms it. The distinction is between a practitioner who does the work for the team (delivery person) and a practitioner who does the work with the team in a way that builds the team's capability to do it themselves (capability builder).

**Verbatim quote (Jess):** "This is not me going off to do solo work for you. I do it with you, not for you or to you."

**The confirmation:** Cheryl confirms in Session 2 that she has been using this framing in her conversations with the operational leaders: "I said, you can't do this in one workshop or one or two days. You do wanna do it over a series. So within a six week period, there'll be three workshops and I'm just about to facilitate. I've designed number two." This is the capability builder in action — not a one-off delivery, but a structured programme designed to build the team's capability over time.

**The ally identification principle:** Jess adds a new dimension in Session 2: "You will be able to find out who would be your ally and the one that you can quickly get a win out of." The capability builder does not just build capability — they identify the early adopters, the allies, the people who will become advocates. The quick win with the right person is the seed that grows into the ambassador effect.

**Content potential:**
- LinkedIn post: "The capability builder vs. the delivery person. The distinction that determines whether you're a resource or a partner."
- Workshop module: Positioning for internal practitioners — the capability builder identity
- Elevate programme: The ally identification framework

**Priority:** High

# The Five Shifts of Modern Change Practice, Hyper-Personalised Adoption, and AI as Amplifier — IP Extraction
## Blog: "Change needs changing" | transformationleadership.institute
**Source context:** A LinkedIn newsletter blog post by Jess Tayel addressing what has gone stale in change and transformation practice and articulating five concrete shifts needed. Written in direct, practitioner-to-practitioner voice. Published as part of the Transformation Leadership Newsletter series.
**Dominant theme:** The Five Shifts — a practical framework for modernising change and transformation practice
**Secondary themes:** AI as amplifier (not the change itself); hyper-personalised adoption; culture as non-negotiable; the distinction between having change professionals on a programme vs. embedding change adoption design from day one
**Sessions this theme also appears in:** Blog 10 ("Change Management is boring") — both address the credibility crisis of change practice; Cheryl Session 1 — the "arms and legs" distinction connects to the shift from delivery-support to embedded practice
**Extracted by:** Manus AI | March 2026
**Confidential — Jess Internal Document**

---

### The Five Shifts of Modern Change Practice

**Source:** "Change needs changing" blog | transformationleadership.institute | Section: "What needs to change in change — The 5 Shifts"

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "The old playbook of linear plans, communications, and training, along with methodology debates, can't carry the speed, scale, and messiness that organisations are already facing. Here's what I'm seeing, and what actually helps."
> "Move from 'stakeholders' to specific humans in specific jobs. Map the few critical behaviours by role and moment. Measure usable adoption (is the new way used in the flow of work? If not, why?)"
> "Turn concepts into micro-practices people can try today. Use AI for the unglamorous heavy lifting: segment communications by role, draft job-specific 'first-run' guides, summarise feedback channels, and surface friction signals in real-time. AI is not the change; it's the amplifier."

**What this IP is:**
A five-part framework that replaces the outdated change management playbook with a modern, integrated approach. Each shift addresses a specific failure mode in conventional practice. Together they form a complete operating upgrade for any change or transformation leader who wants their work to actually land and stick — not just be delivered.

**Who it is for:**
Senior change and transformation practitioners who are frustrated that their technically correct work is not producing adoption. Programme leaders who are running conventional change plans and watching them underperform. Executives who have funded change and are not seeing the results they were promised.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The conventional change playbook — stakeholder maps, communications calendars, training sessions, methodology debates — is not producing adoption at the speed and scale organisations now need. Practitioners know something is wrong but are not sure what to replace it with. The result is good people doing the right things in the wrong sequence, at the wrong level of personalisation, without the culture levers that make change stick.

**Benefits / value add:**
The Five Shifts give practitioners a concrete upgrade path. Each shift is actionable, not aspirational. When embedded, they increase adoption rates significantly — Jess cites approximately 40% adoption increase in programmes that embed these moves. They also reduce transformation friction and give practitioners a language for explaining why their approach is different from the conventional model.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that the problem is not effort — it is architecture. Most change practitioners are working very hard using the wrong design. The Five Shifts are not about doing more; they are about redesigning what you do. The most provocative shift is Shift 4: the distinction between having a change professional on the programme from day one versus having change adoption design embedded from day one. Jess calls this "like night and day" — and most practitioners who read it will recognise they have been doing the former while believing they were doing the latter.

**The framework:**

Shift 1 — Hyper-personalise adoption. Move from "stakeholders" as a category to specific humans in specific jobs. Map the few critical behaviours by role and moment. Measure usable adoption: is the new way actually being used in the flow of work? If not, why not? This shift moves change from a broadcast activity to a precision intervention.

Shift 2 — Fewer concepts, more practice, plus AI where it helps. Turn concepts into micro-practices people can try today — not in a training room, but in the actual flow of work. Use AI for the unglamorous heavy lifting: segment communications by role, draft job-specific "first-run" guides, summarise feedback channels, surface friction signals in real-time. The critical distinction: AI is not the change. It is the amplifier. This prevents the common mistake of treating AI adoption as the transformation goal.

Shift 3 — No change without culture, no culture without change. Make three non-negotiable cultural behaviours explicit. Tie them to decisions, rituals, and consequences. No change will ever be adopted if the culture has not shifted. And no culture will ever evolve without intentional change interventions that address culture directly. These two disciplines must be integrated, not sequential.

Shift 4 — Start change on day one, not close to go-live. Embed adoption design in discovery. Co-create ways of working with the people who will live them. Replace long-form training with in-flow enablement and micro-learnings: pilots, shadowing, checklists at the moment of need, quick feedback loops. The critical distinction (Jess's own emphasis): having a change professional on the programme from day one is not the same as having change adoption and change experience design embedded in the programme from day one. This distinction is like night and day.

Shift 5 — Build capability, not just awareness. Upskill people's capabilities — not just technical training, but also behavioural: navigating complex environments, protecting boundaries and wellbeing. Grow organisational capabilities that remove friction (data literacy, decision rights, customer journey view) so the change does not grind against the system.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Workshops:** The Five Shifts is a complete workshop module. Each shift can be a 20-minute deep-dive with a diagnostic question and a practical exercise.
- **Talks:** A keynote structured around "what has gone stale and what actually helps" — the Five Shifts as the answer.
- **The book:** A chapter titled "The Five Shifts" — the upgrade from the old playbook to the modern practice architecture.
- **Online content:** A LinkedIn post series (one per shift, five posts) with a practical diagnostic question at the end of each.
- **Client work:** Use as a programme design audit — score the current programme against each shift and identify the gaps.
- **GTL Programme:** Shift 4 (the day-one distinction) is a core module in the practice pillar.

**Soundbite:**
> "AI is not the change. It's the amplifier."

**Practical application:**
Run a 10-minute audit using Jess's own checklist from the blog: Who must do what differently by when (one sentence per role)? Where does the old habit still win? What tolerated behaviour makes candour unsafe? Which two micro-practices will help this week? How will you see adoption in the wild (three signals)? This audit can be run by a practitioner alone, with a sponsor, or with a team.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- Shift 4 is the most commonly misunderstood. Many practitioners believe they are doing it because they are "on the programme from day one." The test is whether adoption design — not just change presence — is embedded in the discovery and shaping phases.
- Shift 3 requires naming specific behaviours, not values. "Psychological safety" is not a behaviour. "Decide in the room and say the hard thing early" is a behaviour.
- Shift 2's AI guidance is deliberately narrow: AI for the unglamorous heavy lifting, not for the human work. This is a positioning distinction that matters when speaking to sceptical executives.

**Domain tags:**
Practice — specifically the architecture of modern change practice; also touches Positioning (how a practitioner explains their approach) and Leadership (culture and behaviour design)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 10 ("Change Management is boring") — the Five Shifts are the answer to the credibility problem described there. The two blogs form a paired argument: here is what is broken, here is how to fix it.
- Cheryl Session 1 — "Don't be the arms and legs" connects to Shift 4: being embedded in design, not just supporting delivery.
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer in your 2026 plan") — the Conditions of Success Runway is the programme-level expression of the same principles the Five Shifts address at the practitioner level.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "The change management playbook is broken. Not because practitioners are bad at their jobs. Because the playbook was designed for a world that no longer exists."
Story: "I've been watching the same patterns repeat across organisations for years. Stakeholder maps that don't move people. Training sessions that don't change behaviour. Communications calendars that tick boxes but don't shift anything. The problem isn't effort. It's architecture."
Insight: "Here are the five shifts that actually work: hyper-personalise adoption, fewer concepts and more practice, no change without culture, start on day one (not go-live), and build capability not just awareness. The most important one? The distinction between having a change professional on the programme from day one and having change adoption design embedded from day one. That distinction is like night and day."
CTA: "Which of these five shifts is your programme missing? Comment below — I read every response."

**Written excerpt / carousel idea:**
Opening: "AI is not the change. It's the amplifier." — use as a standalone post or carousel slide. Five slides, one shift per slide, with a diagnostic question at the bottom of each.

### The Day-One Distinction — Change Presence vs. Change Design

**Source:** "Change needs changing" blog | Section: Shift 4 | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "If you have read the title of this point, #4, and you already know you are doing this (engaging change early), I invite you to look deeper into the distinction between having a change professional/team on the program from day 1 and having change adoption and change experience design embedded in the program from day 1. This distinction is like night and day."

**What this IP is:**
A precision distinction that separates two things that look identical from the outside but produce completely different results. Having a change professional present from day one is a staffing decision. Having change adoption design embedded from day one is an architectural decision. Most programmes do the former and believe they are doing the latter.

**Who it is for:**
Change directors and programme leads who are confident they are doing change "right" because they have a change team on the programme from the start. Executives who are sponsoring transformation and want to understand why their change investment is not producing the adoption they expected.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The frustration of doing everything "correctly" — having change resources, running the workshops, producing the plans — and still not seeing adoption. The distinction explains why: presence is not the same as design. The change team is there, but the adoption architecture is not embedded in the programme's shaping and discovery phases.

**Benefits / value add:**
This distinction gives practitioners a precise language for a conversation they have been unable to have. It also gives sponsors a diagnostic question: "Is our change team present, or is change adoption design embedded?" The answer to that question predicts the programme's adoption outcome more accurately than any other single factor.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that having a change team from day one can actually create a false sense of security. The organisation believes it has "done change" because the change team is there. But if adoption design is not embedded in the discovery and shaping phases, the change team is just watching the problem being created rather than preventing it.

**The framework:**
Two states: (1) Change presence — a change professional or team is on the programme from day one, attending meetings, producing plans, running stakeholder maps. (2) Change design embedded — adoption is designed into the programme architecture from discovery: who must do what differently is mapped before the solution is built; the culture and behaviour levers are identified before go-live; the in-flow enablement is designed before training is scheduled. The test: if the change team were removed from the programme tomorrow, would the adoption design still be embedded in the programme's architecture? If yes, you have done Shift 4. If no, you have change presence, not change design.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Diagnostic tool:** Use as a single-question diagnostic in a programme health check.
- **Talks:** A 5-minute segment in any keynote on transformation practice.
- **Client work:** Use to reframe a conversation with a sponsor who believes change is "covered" because there is a change team.
- **GTL Programme:** A core module in the Practice pillar — the distinction between being on the programme and being embedded in the programme.

**Soundbite:**
> "Having a change professional on the programme from day one and having change adoption design embedded from day one — that distinction is like night and day."

**Practical application:**
Ask three questions: (1) Is adoption designed into the discovery phase, or is it planned for later? (2) Are the critical behaviours by role identified before the solution is built, or after? (3) Is the culture work integrated with the delivery work, or is it a separate workstream? If the answer to any of these is "after" or "separate," the programme has change presence, not change design.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- This distinction is most powerful when used with a sponsor who is resistant to investing in change. It reframes the conversation from "do you have change resources?" to "is change designed in?"
- The distinction also applies to culture work: having a culture programme running alongside transformation is not the same as having culture designed into the transformation.

**Domain tags:**
Practice — the architecture of change adoption design; also Positioning (how practitioners explain their value)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer") — the Conditions of Success Runway is the programme-level version of this distinction. Component 3 (Change Enablement) is where this distinction lives at the programme design level.
- Blog 10 ("Change Management is boring") — Pattern 1 ("Change as a companion to delivery") is the organisational expression of the same problem this distinction addresses at the practitioner level.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "You have a change team on the programme from day one. That's not the same as having change designed in."
Story: "I've seen this pattern more times than I can count. The change team is present. They're attending the meetings. They're producing the plans. And yet, when the programme reaches go-live, adoption is lower than expected. The reason is almost always this distinction."
Insight: "Having a change professional present from day one is a staffing decision. Having change adoption design embedded from day one is an architectural decision. The test: if the change team were removed tomorrow, would the adoption design still be in the programme? If not, you have presence, not design."
CTA: "Which one does your programme have? Be honest."
